Because it is a custom system, it cannot use binary python packages made with Raspbian in mind; because it is an arm7hf architecture, it cannot use more general manylinux wheels made with x86-64 or arm64 in mind. Since the OT-2 itself does not have a compiler, we use this repository to provide prebuilt python wheels that are installable on an OT-2 by using a [PEP-503](https://peps.python.org/pep-0503) simple package index.\\n\\nAny package identified here can be installed on an OT-2 through `pip`.\\n\\n## Requesting Packages\\n...\\n## Building, and Dependencies To Build\\n...\\n## Build Tools\\n...\\n## Adding a New Package\\n...\\n## How does this all work, anyway?\\n...\\n```File: `README.md`\\n```markdown\\n# opentrons-python-packages\\n...\\n## Build Tools\\n\\nThe build tools are a separate package that is developed in this repo in `./tools` - see that directory for more.\\n\\n...\\n```File: `README.md`\\n```markdown\\n# opentrons-python-packages\\n...\\n## Building, and Dependencies To Build\\n\\nThis repo is intended to work in production on github CI, but you can test builds locally (and should, since some packages can have surprising build system requirements). The entry point for building is `./build-packages`. It's a python script that should work on anything above python 3.7. You can get usage by running `./build-packages --help`.\\n\\nYou'll also need to have Docker installed and running and accessible with how you call `./build-packages` (i.e., if you don't want to use `sudo` then the docker socket should be user-accessible).\\n\\n### Machine Compatibility for Building\\n...\\n```File: `README.md`\\n```markdown\\n# opentrons-python-packages\\n...\\n## How does this all work, anyway?\\n\\nSee the README.md in `tools/`!\\n\\n```File: `README.md`\\n```markdown\\n# opentrons-python-packages\\n...\\n## Adding a New Package\\n...\\n- a git tag to find\\n- some other options about how the package is distributed and what kind of source it is. `tools/README.md`\\n````markdown\\n# Build Tools\\n...\\n## How does this all work, anyway?\\n\\nThe core of the problem this package solves is building python packages for arm7hf on an x86_64 machine - cross compiling them. To do this it takes a lot of cues and support from buildroot.\\n\\nWe need a stable environment for the compilation, and we'll be messing with shell settings, so we do the work in docker. That means we have code that has to run in docker (container side) and code that has to run outside of docker, to manage docker (host-side).\\n\\n### Host side\\n...\\n### Container side\\n...\\n````File: `tools/README.md`\\n````markdown\\n# Build Tools\\n...\\n## Testing Build Tools\\n\\nThe top-level `./build` script entrypoint is going to mostly try to download the latest release of the build tools docker container rather than use your local stuff. It also lives in a top level directory which is annoying to use. To test locally you can use the poetry entrypoint `poetry poe run-build`.\\n\\n## Testing Content\\n\\nOnce you do a package build, you can see how it looked by doing `poetry run serve PORT` where you specify a port. You'll have to set the port in the build flags.\\n\\n...\\n````File: `tools/README.md`\\n````markdown\\n# Build Tools\\n\\nThis subdirectory holds the local build tools we use for building the python packages. They are responsible for build orchestration and kicking off everything.\\n\\nThis tooling uses [poetry](python-poetry.org) with some plugins:\\n- [poetry-dynamic-versioning](https://pypi.org/project/poetry-dynamic-versioning/). to take versions from git\\n- [poe-the-poet](https://pypi.org/project/poethepoet/) to run tasks like test\\n\\nYou don't need to install these to _use_ the tooling - docker takes care of that - but you do need to install them to _change_ the tooling.\\n\\nYou do need to install docker for your preferred platform.\\n\\n## Setting up to develop the build tools\\n...\\n## Using Poetry\\n...\\n## Testing Build Tools\\n...\\n## Testing Content\\n...\\n## How does this all work, anyway?\\n...\\n````File: `tools/README.md`\\n````markdown\\n# Build Tools\\n...\\n## How does this all work, anyway?\\n...\\n### Container side\\n\\nThe container side code has to actually build all the packages. This duplicates some of the functionality of buildroot. Its job is to\\n1. Find the package and run its `build.py` (in `builder/package_build/orchestrate.py`)\\n2. Make sure we have everything we need to build the package, such as \\n   - package sources (`builder/package_build/download.py`)\\n   - package build dependencies (`builder/package_build/build_wheel.py`)\\n   - an activated buildroot sdk\\n   - an activated python virtual environment with the build dependencies\\n   - correct environments for forcing python to cross-compile\\n3. Actually run the build and harvest the results (also `build_wheel.py`)\\n\\nThe most complex part of this is making sure there's a correct environment to build things. `tools/README.md`\\n````markdown\\n# Build Tools\\n...\\n## How does this all work, anyway?\\n\\nThe core of the problem this package solves is building python packages for arm7hf on an x86_64 machine - cross compiling them. To do this it takes a lot of cues and support from buildroot.\\n\\nWe need a stable environment for the compilation, and we'll be messing with shell settings, so we do the work in docker. That means we have code that has to run in docker (container side) and code that has to run outside of docker, to manage docker (host-side).\\n\\n### Host side\\n...\\n### Container side\\n...\\n````File: `tools/README.md`\\n````markdown\\n# Build Tools\\n...\\n## How does this all work, anyway?\\n...\\n### Host side\\n\\nThe host-side code is in `builder/host` and `builder/common`. The job of this code is to build or pull docker containers, and then run a docker container with correct flags. That's not a lot, and that's good, because that means we can have approximately 0 dependencies locally. This is why you can run `build-packages` with just python without having to do poetry setup.\\n\\n...\\n````File: `tools/README.md`\\n````markdown\\n# Build Tools\\n...\\n## How does this all work, anyway?\\n...\\n### Container side\\n\\nThe container side code has to actually build all the packages. This duplicates some of the functionality of buildroot. Its job is to\\n1. Find the package and run its `build.py` (in `builder/package_build/orchestrate.py`)\\n2. Make sure we have everything we need to build the package, such as \\n   - package sources (`builder/package_build/download.py`)\\n   - package build dependencies (`builder/package_build/build_wheel.py`)\\n   - an activated buildroot sdk\\n   - an activated python virtual environment with the build dependencies\\n   - correct environments for forcing python to cross-compile\\n3. Actually run the build and harvest the results (also `build_wheel.py`)\\n\\nThe most complex part of this is making sure there's a correct environment to build things. The environment is important because it's the only way to pass certain options to the wheel builder (like the platform it should compile for) and provide cross compilation tools. This is done by having a long-running interactive shell that we communicate with in `builder/package_build/shell_environment.py`. \\n\\n````File: `tools/README.md`\\n````markdown\\n# Build Tools\\n...\\n## Setting up to develop the build tools\\n\\nInstall poetry:\\n\\n``` shell\\ncurl -sSL https://install.python-poetry.org | python3 -\\n```\\n\\nInstall required plugins:\\n\\n``` shell\\n          poetry self add \\\"poetry-dynamic-versioning[plugin]\\\"\\n          poetry self add \\\"poethepoet[poetry_plugin]\\\"\\n```\\n\\nSet up the local environment:\\n\\n``` shell\\npoetry install\\n```\\n\\n## Using Poetry\\n\\nBecause we use poe, we can get away without having a wrapping makefile for dev tasks.\\n\\nYou can\\n\\n- *set up*: using `poetry install`\\n- *lint*: using `poetry poe lint`\\n- *format*: using `poetry poe format`\\n- *test*: using `poetry poe test`\\n- *try a build*: using `poetry poe run-build`\\n\\n...\\n````\",\"languageName\":\"Markdown\",\"path\":\"tools/README.md\",\"range\":{\"start\":13,\"end\":76},\"ref\":\"refs/heads/main\",\"type\":\"snippet\",\"url\":\"https://github.com/sgbaird/opentrons-python-packages/blob/2fa39d1cfe8be45deaad5cd8dd18a8d3b97940e3/tools/README.md#L13-L76\"}]"

2025-06-13T16:56:41.3637182Z     124.
2025-06-13T16:56:41.3637780Z     125.        return code detection only really works if the return code of cmd is
2025-06-13T16:56:41.3638594Z     126.        relevant to the main thing that happens in cmd. that means that cmd shouldn't
2025-06-13T16:56:41.3639352Z     127.        have || clauses and really should just have one actual command.

2025-06-13T16:56:41.3646696Z     144.            if match := self._result_re.search(stdout):
2025-06-13T16:56:41.3647222Z     145.                return int(match.group(1)), stdout_lines

2025-06-13T17:06:52.3380719Z   "prTitle": "Fix pandas complex number compilation failure in cross-compilation environment",
2025-06-13T17:06:52.3393108Z   "prDescription": "## Problem\n\nThe CI was failing when building pandas 1.5.0 for ARM architecture due to compilation errors in `pandas/_libs/src/klib/khash_python.h`. The error occurred because the `KHASH_MAP_INIT_COMPLEX64` and `KHASH_MAP_INIT_COMPLEX128` macros were trying to access `.real` and `.imag` members on objects that didn't have those fields during cross-compilation.\n\n## Root Cause\n\nThe cross-compilation environment was missing proper complex number type definitions for the ARM target platform. When pandas attempted to compile its C extensions using complex number macros, the compiler couldn't find the necessary complex number support.\n\n## Solution\n\nAdded comprehensive complex number compiler flags to the cross-compilation environment in `initiate_python_environment()`:\n\n```python\n# Fix for pandas complex number compilation issue\n# Add compiler flags to ensure proper complex number support for ARM cross-compilation\ncomplex_flags = \"-D_Complex_I=I -D_GNU_SOURCE -std=gnu99\"\nself._guarded_shellcall(f\"export CFLAGS=\\\"$CFLAGS {complex_flags}\\\"\")\nself._guarded_shellcall(f\"export CPPFLAGS=\\\"$CPPFLAGS {complex_flags}\\\"\")\n```\n\n**Flags added:**\n- `-D_Complex_I=I`: Defines the complex number constant I\n- `-D_GNU_SOURCE`: Enables GNU C library extensions for better compatibility  \n- `-std=gnu99`: Ensures C99 complex number support\n\nThese flags are applied to both `CFLAGS` and `CPPFLAGS` to ensure comprehensive coverage during compilation.\n\n## Impact\n\nThis fix ensures that pandas khash macros can properly access complex number members (`.real` and `.imag`) during ARM cross-compilation, resolving the build failures in CI.\n\n## Testing\n\n- Created comprehensive unit tests validating environment setup\n- Verified all complex number flags are properly applied\n- Confirmed no regressions in existing functionality\n- Validated the fix addresses the specific pandas compilation issue\n\nFixes #7."
